ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u20	We are starting the debate with the rapporteur-general from New Democracy, Mr Pericles Manda. Mr. Mada, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u21	Thank you very much, Mr President. Minister, Secretary-General, ladies and gentlemen, today I have the pleasure and honour of introducing a pioneering and innovative bill, a bill primarily aimed at the world of work. A bill that introduces stability, confidence and security. A bill aimed solely at the new generation, to all those who, with appetite and ambition, start working life and who have learned that decent pensions are unfortunately something that is not there and their contributions are a forced and unjust expenditure that the state imposes on pensioners today. Who can claim that things are not like this when from their money there is not a single euro left for them, but everything leads to a large basket, to a corpse for the current pensioners? So the problem has become clear for some time and has two legs: on the one hand there is the demographic ageing that plagues the entire developed world. And it is true that as long as conditions and life expectancy are improved, as long as medicine and science advances and as long as our social state grows, insurance and pension distribution systems are pressed and the more the globe will become in favour of the pensioners concerned and at the expense of every new worker. It is precisely this phenomenon that gives us the second part of the problem, the one-dimensional and rigid, the completely and completely distribution system that we have today, which operates well in different circumstances and not in what we have today. When the present system was founded in the 1950s, the ratio of workers to pensioners was four to one and it was true that it was working well at that time. Today, however, the data are different and that ratio has not existed for years. Let us not hide behind words and see things straight and honest. As long as the contributions of the workers concerned are not saved, but are spent on the current pensions, the young people are becoming the major losers of a system that is stable and inextricably depreciated. Who doesn't see it anyway? In our country they are increasingly working to sustain more and less. And that's the big truth. We must therefore respond to this dual problem, read the data, study the data and provide sound and long-term solutions. That is exactly what today's bill is doing. With a sense of responsibility and full responsibility, with political determination and social sensitivity, looking after both the young people insured and the existing pensioners and offering guarantees wherever and whenever necessary, but also by establishing an increase in supplementary pensions, together with, of course, the development of our national economy. What, then, does the draft law on the new subsidiary introduce? For any new insured person who is liable for subsidiary insurance, but also for those who are up to thirty-five years old today and who optionally choose from 1-1-2022 in step and gradually convert the subsidiary insurance system from distribution to capitalisation. It is, in other words, the institution of the individual piggyback, so that everyone's contributions are theirs and that they are no longer brought to the common corps. And as savings in each individual account and money gradually increase an important chapter accumulates a step - step, which is investing, increasing and yielding, while strengthening our national economy in a mature, documented and secure way. This is what almost all the countries of the developed world are doing by introducing capitalising characteristics into their pension systems with measure and wisdom. That is what we are doing, maintaining both the public nature of subsidiary insurance and its compulsoryity. We are therefore following the example of the Nordic countries and avoiding the failed models of Latin America and the former Eastern bloc which have fully capitalised their own social security and led the system to crystallised inequalities and ultimately to a definitive collapse. At the same time, transparency, accountability, control and supervision are introduced. The new fund, established by the TECA, implements investment policies and governance practices that we are used to seeing only in the private economy, making use of the money and contributions of the insured for the benefit of themselves and not some others. An online and real-time monitoring of investments and portfolios is enacted. An online platform that looks like the internet banking is created and the insured is able to choose the investment profile that suits his preferences, thus giving better control over the final amount of the supplementary pension he receives. At the same time, two important guarantees are introduced for both pensioners and old insured persons and for young people. First of all, there is nothing to change about the existing members because those who are already part of the supplementary insurance system remain as they are without the slightest change, and only if they want and are up to thirty-five years old can change the new system. And that's something most likely many of our young people will choose. At the same time, current pensioners are not in any way affected, because it is clearly predicted that any cost of the transition, although fully manageable and low on the basis of studies, will always be covered by the state budget. A second guarantee, however, is introduced especially for young insured persons, which ensures that even in the extreme case that something is wrong and their individual pigeon is found with less money than they gave through their contributions, they will in any event receive at least as much supplementary pension as their contributions plus inflation. At the same time, it is predicted that if an insured person fails to complete 15 years of insurance and is therefore not entitled to a pension, his contributions are reimbursed, and so far they would be lost and fruited by others. Some may wonder. Is the contributions increased? Indicatively not, because the level of the contributions remains at 6.5% by the middle of 2022 and then goes to 6% where it stays from there and beyond. Is subsidiary insurance privatised? Incidentally not, because the new subsidiary is supported by the new fund, and this is public like e-ECA and other public funds. So we're setting up another public body? Not even in this case, because the new Capital Insurance Fund may be established as a State institution, but is almost fully managed by high-educational executives, with special internal regulations, with a high level of operation and, of course, with technocratic competence. In other words, the TECA operates in a context of strict governance rules, thereby ensuring a equally strict risk management framework to the benefit of its insured and its pensioners. Ladies and gentlemen, how did the Opposition stand against all this? How did the Opposition assess the prospect of increasing pensions and strengthening and strengthening tomorrow's insured person? Unfortunately, once again, we have seen misery, opposition, fearfulness and catastrophe, despite the fact that three studies from well-known and authoritative bodies have certified the right direction of the bill and despite the open support received from teachers and former ministers. And what we have not heard, ladies and gentlemen, over the last few years. We heard that the Commission was supposed to object, that new taxes were supposed to come, that the figures did not come and that the social reactions were scaring us. But what social reactions are these and where do they come from? Are they from those who will see increases in their supplementary pensions, from those who now have more choices and supervision and accountability for where their money goes, or from those who do not like state guarantees and want to protest about it? We were also - it says - inconsistent, because it is not possible for the insurance system to be sustainable, as the then Minister Brutsis, in 2020, said, but also to need reform, as is the case with today's bill. Because, in other words, Mr Brutsis reformed the law on an abortion of Mr Katrugalos and changed the framework for the main pensions - and it is clear that he did very well - and gave a breath to thousands of self-employed people and businesses, all of a sudden today we are not allowed to want social security to be made fairer, more efficient and better in the area of subsidiary insurance. It's funny, of course, that they can't stand criticism. We have also heard the concerns about the cost of transition, that it is alleged that no one can predict or prejudge, that growth will be weak and that the cost of transition will be high. All right, but where's all this going? There was some research or some study and it wasn't called? Why does the studies carried out by three trustworthy and emblematic organisations say others that do not support their own concerns and fears? Ladies and gentlemen, the government does not arbitraryly legislate and according to what has been done. The government built the bill on real facts, in real studies, with facts which it also attached to the bill, so that there is no doubt about it. The conclusions not only confirmed the right direction of the draft law, but on the substance they formed the full background on which it is based. The cost of transition is at 56 billion and the benefit to the national economy of about 50 billion. So the cost to the State is about EUR 120 million per year. It was certified by the proportional study of the National Analogue Authority, with the prestige and gravity that distinguishes it, as we say from the outset. That is the reality because this is based on real elements. On the other hand, there is a study by IOBE, which, in eighteen different scenarios, concluded that due to the reform of subsidiary insurance, the country is leading to an increase in GDP and employment, with a positive impact on the country's fiscal and development figures, with full annual coverage of the transition costs since 2032 and then cumulative coverage since 2040. Within ten years, that is, the new subsidiary insurance framework introduced by the government can fully compensate for the annual cost of transition and then act as a lever for the benefit of both its insured and our national economy. While, of course, the third study accompanying the bill, the DPRK's debt bill, clearly substantiates that there is no question of any issue with the country's debt. Because in all the scenarios examined, from the most optimistic to the most pessimistic, the results of the analysis were undoubtedly positive. It is thus confirmed that the reform is really gradual, balanced and backward, avoiding sharp changes and major turbulence and ultimately leading to the reduction of public debt by approximately 6.3% of GDP in 2070. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, this is a way of dealing with budgets rather than with fragmentary expressions of "one law and one article" or with "dogs and barrens". Bonds and mutuals are not gamblers and sinners, as many MPs in the counter-population know very well. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, in order to assess the reform of supplementary insurance sought by the government, we must respond manually to the heart. Is this reform in the interest of young people ultimately in the interests of young people? Is their prospects improved or worse? Is it or is it not mortal the current fully distributive system that we are implementing? And how can we ultimately guarantee future pensions better? I think that the answers to all of this are self-evident for all of us, even for those who prefer for ideological, opposition or other reasons to be against any new, pioneering and reformist, because in this bill for the first time over the last thirty years, a structural change in subsidiary insurance is being attempted. So justly, this reform takes historical proportions, touching the most creative, the most productive, the most developmental part of Greek society, the new generation. Thank you.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u22	The rapporteur for SYRIZA will now be speaking, Mrs Maria - Elisa Xinoyannakopoulos.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u23	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the draft law on the capitalisation system in the supplementary insurance system that we are debating today is completely integrated - I would say that it is also a basic pillar - in the overall policy of the Government of New Democracy, a policy that is radically neoliberal - with outdated and failed recipes, a policy that systematically seeks - with a climax, of course, the famous 'Hajidakis' for labour - to make our country of cheap and flexible labour, deregulation of everything and gradual degradation of the social state in all its appearances. We have seen it in education with the characteristic, dramatic consequences of the thousands of excluded students from higher education institutions, who are, of course, extinguished to private education and colleges, we have seen it in the National Health System, although there has been a slowdown in privatization due to the pandemic. However, the target remains. ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u44	Ladies and gentlemen, as I have said before, we have a hundred and twenty speakers. I would therefore ask that we show respect for other speakers. Mr. Bougas has the floor. Please also speak within five minutes. You have the floor, Mr Buga.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u45	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, I will speak directly about the objection of unconstitutionality. So this objection is manifestly unfounded. The arguments that make up the historical basis of the objection are not supported by constitutional law scientists. They have been judged and rejected by the case-law of the Council of State and have been challenged by a recent ad hoc opinion of the Court of Auditors. Social security is compulsory under our Constitution and is public, i.e. provided by the State or legal entities governed by public law. The consolidated, ladies and gentlemen, case-law of the Supreme Courts since 1975 has clarified precisely the obligation of the joint legislature not to harm the hard core of the right, namely the sustainability of the whole system, but also to ensure decent living. What is the result of studying the provisions of the draft law we are discussing today? The introduction of the social security grid requires a social security institution as a State entity, i.e. a legal person governed by public law, even under strict state supervision by the National Accounting Authority, the Capital Market Commission and the Ministry of Labour. The great advantage, however, of the draft law under discussion is to provide for the use of the capitalisation system. Under the current circumstances the introduction of the capitalisation system in supplementary pensions will make a significant contribution to the so-called trans-national justice and trust of pensioners. Only thus will the intrinsic injustice resulting from the inevitable ageing of the population be removed. The younger groups of insured persons will certainly be smaller than the previous ones. As the supplementary pension is based on equality of contributions and benefits, each new pensioner group will henceforth take less than the previous group and certainly less than the contributions paid. This opposition seems to be legally accepted. We do not accept it. The draft law ensures that the actual total amount of the insured person’s pension will not be less than its total contributions, but in principle it will be considerably higher. From this provision of the draft law, the main argument of opposition to unconstitutionalism is to be overcome. All the European countries that currently face similar problems, and to a lesser extent compared to Greece, provide for the combination of the distribution and capitalisation system in order to make use of the combination of the advantages of one model and the other. The editorial legislator laid down the State’s sovereign obligations and granted the joint legislature the choice of the instruments by drawing up specific proportionate and economic studies, so that the public purpose, i.e. the overall sustainability of the system and a decent living, is actually achieved each time. It is therefore possible to apply a purely distribution system, a purely capitalist or, as is the case in many states, a mixed system. Further, of course, there is no pilot run. A capitalisation system has already been in force since 2013 in compulsory subsidiary social security. As you know, four compulsory occupational insurance funds have been set up and operate as you know. The Council of State, in order to come to SYRIZA's arguments, confirmed the agreement with the Constitution both with the Decision 960 of 2017 and with the recent Decision 2482 of 2020, which seems to validate overall the political choice of the capitalisation of subsidiary insurance. I have to say in respect of the decision invoked by SYRIZA that Decision 1889 of 2019 of the Plenary Assembly is not differentiated, ladies and gentlemen, as far as the criteria are concerned, but as regards the wording. That is why the subsequent 2020 decision, which I mentioned before, also in view of the 2019 plenary decision, confirms - I repeat - the overall policy of the capitalisation of subsidiary insurance. For the Minutes, I present all the decisions of the Council of State which accept all the constitutionality of the relevant arrangements. I do not mention them, Mr President, for the sake of brevity. [[At this point, the Member, Mr Ioannis Bougas, presents for the minutes the above-mentioned documents, which are in the archives of the Secretariat of the Directorate for Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] To conclude, Mr President, I must say that so far experience of the operation of the four funds mentioned above has been very positive and takes into account the demographic, economic and other indicators for the adequacy of the system's viability. Furthermore, in order to fulfil the conditions set out in the case-law of the Council of State, I refer to the provisions of Articles 59 and 60 of the draft law with the double state guarantee for old and new insured persons. I have no time to refer to the ad hoc opinion of the Court of Auditors. In saying a few words about it, however, I must tell you that, in view of the findings of the case-law, the Court of Auditors accepts that the proposed system is fully in line with the Constitution if the conditions met in the draft law are met. For these reasons, I urge everyone to vote against the objection of unconstitutionality with the provisions of the bill and to support unanimously the important reform that we at last dare for the benefit of the social group, not only for today, but also for tomorrow in our country. Thank you very much.

The contributions paid by each new worker will go to their own pension and will not be used to pay the supplementary pension for the current pensioners, as they currently stand. And from the examples so far, we can conclude that they are successful models. Ladies and gentlemen, the transformation of the country cannot be achieved through words and philosophical debates on how to be better. We also need political decisions. The bill we are called upon to vote on today is one of these necessary changes that will substantially contribute to changing Greece and creating a better future for our children. That is why I call on you to support this legislative initiative by the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs by voting in favour of, finally, supporting young people and not, once again, being against them. The key point of the major reform is to transform part of the current social security system from distribution to capitalisation. In other words, introducing the institution of individual money to ensure that the contributions of each newly insured person rather than being channelled as it has been in a common corps in favour of current pensioners are stored in separate individual accounts and can by decision of the insured persons themselves be invested on the basis of three investment profiles. So the insured not only will feel that their money has a dynamic within the time that can provide them with more choice but also better control over the final supplementary pension they receive, but also the national economy will also be supported, since much of the money collected will be invested in the country in the example of the majority of OECD countries that successfully implement similar supplementary capitalisation schemes, without jeopardising the compulsory public security. Ladies and gentlemen, the New Democracy Government, leaving the sloppies and genographies behind in SYRIZA, today brings a bill on subsidiary insurance, the adequacy and necessity of which are documented by three studies: a proportionality, a macroeconomic and a sustainability of public debt. With these studies and good practices at international level, we propose a reform that disconnects supplementary pensions from demographic developments and the gradual ageing of the Greek population, which creates a new independent and stable insurance pillar with a significantly greater degree of reciprocity and freedom, and finally a reform that causes multiple and combined benefits for the insured and for our national economy. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u108	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, we are called upon to vote on an insurance reform that has eyes turned solely on the new generation of workers. This draft law attempts to radically reform the architecture of the public subsidiary insurance system by gradually introducing for the first time a capitalisation system for the financial operation of insurance contributions in its place until now an existing distribution system. The planned reform is a necessary and inevitable innovation for the Greek reality, which has been delayed and can no longer be postponed. Demographic trends in the whole world are particularly noticeable, and in our country they lead with mathematical accuracy to a reduction in the ratio between active insured and retired persons, which prescribes as a fatal consequence the ingestion of supplementary pensions. This conclusion is based on unresolved mathematical calculations and is not a product of ideological payments. The obsession with the distribution system, namely the payment of the pensions paid from the annual contributions in the hope of the active people, that the next generation will do the same for them is on the basis of demographic correlations actually a utopia. Therefore, the issue in this bill is not to assess the correctness of the transition of subsidiary insurance from distribution to capitalising, because this option is dictated by all economic and sociological findings. It is a question of shaping all these conditions that will make the planned transition effective in practice, reliable to citizens and sustainable over time. In this context, I would like to point out that this draft law is introduced together with a proportionate and macroeconomic study, as well as an analysis of the sustainability of the public debt. Therefore, in each version the proposed solution is fully documented and financially audited so that it does not leave any room for doubt on its long-term positive pension footprint. With regard to the scope of the reform, this draft law, firstly, relates to subsidiary insurance, i.e. the second pillar of social security, which operates periodically and is required to contribute to ensuring the level of living complement to main insurance. It is not, therefore, about or in any way about the formation of the main pensions. Secondly, it does not concern and does not affect existing pensions and current pensioners. As has already been said, it is compulsory for new entrants on the labour market from 1-1 to 2022, and on a voluntary basis for workers aged between 30 and 30. In other words, this is a reform aimed solely at the new generation of workers, as we have said. The innovation of the new system is essentially the creation of individual accounts bearing the name of each insured person and the possibility of investing the contributions collected. Installation of money into investment portfolios is attributed to a pension which is increased depending on the level of returns. This investment option is compensated by the State guarantee, which ensures that the pensioner receives in any case a pension corresponding to the actual value of the contributions paid. In this context, with the legislative proposal that we are discussing today, we are exempted from the demographic risk of the distribution system and are attributed to them the investment benefits of the capitalisation system without risking the loss of the contributions they have paid. This is how it establishes a public and truly liberal system. The implementation of this reform is entrusted to a newly established insurance institution, the Auxiliary and Capital Insurance Fund. The investment activity of that entity, subject to the principle of sound prudent management, to the diversification of the portfolio and the dispersion of investment risk, together with the establishment of an investment committee set up by certified capital managers, creates a structured framework of risk-sensitive investment capacity and the possibilities to ensure stable returns. The same proportional study on which our proposal is based in three different scenarios in terms of the evolution of the human anthropogeography of social security provides for an average nominal return on investment which can reach between 11% and 13% higher than today, while in the full evolution of the new system the rate of increase can be even higher. The Auxiliary and Capital Insurance Fund should become the model that will foster the confidence of the insured in the State's ability to guarantee their property and work life, as well as create the belief that the relationship of a citizen is both two-way and beneficial. Ladies and gentlemen, the provision of decent pension benefits is at the heart of the social state. The data on which the uninterrupted performance of the pension entitlement depends dictate the need to adjust the supplementary pension scheme to new bases. The stagnation of the distribution system which condemns subsidiary insurance in certain marbles gives its place in this bill of law to a fairer and more dynamic system for exploiting the capital of the working life of citizens. Staying public and socially liberal, subsidiary insurance takes a new breath and demands the confidence of workers in the insurance system. That is why I am voting in favour of this bill. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u112	Thank you, Mr President. Mr Minister, ladies and gentlemen, we used to say that subgenitality and demographic problem are a night bomb on the foundations of Greek society. And not just Greek society. Europe and the Western world. I have the feeling that this bomb has now secured itself and, indeed, our society and our insurance system are at risk, because fewer and fewer enter the labour market and we are trying to support more and more people in retirement. And here are two relentless questions: Is there a feeling that the present insurance system is sufficient? Can he support both workers and pensioners over time? And the other question is if the new generation, our new fellow citizens trust the present insurance system and they estimate that they can have a decent pension. Because I have the feeling that you all agree that these two questions are being answered negatively, our Ministry and our Minister are coming with this draft law which sets the following four objectives: first, to restore the stability of the insurance system. Secondly, to strengthen saving. Thirdly, the creation of higher pensions for the young insured and fourth and most importantly, to restore the confidence of the young generation, not only in the insurance system but also in the Greek economy. Ladies and gentlemen, the draft law we are debating today is a bill that introduces greater stability, greater security, more confidence in our insurance system. And most importantly, we all have to recognise that this bill focuses on the new generation, focuses on tomorrow, in the future of our society, focuses on tomorrow's Greece. So we are going ahead with the path that has already been followed by other developed countries, as I said before, because they too are facing the same problem of birth and demographic decline, such as Sweden, Denmark and the Netherlands. In short, we are not apprentices of magicians, but good practice, good tactics that have been tested, and we in our country do. In fact, this system has been shown to lead to much higher pensions than 43%, as estimated, up to 68%. So we have been following models that have been in place for decades in a multitude of European countries and are estimated to lead to higher supplementary pensions for young insured persons, without of course affecting older people or existing pensioners. The central idea of the new system is the introduction of elements of a capitalisation system to supplementary pensions for both new entrants from 1-1-2022 who are liable for subsidiary insurance and volunteering for employees and self-employed persons up to thirty-five years old. Thus, instead of the insurance contributions of young people working for the payment of supplementary pensions for existing pensioners, the contributions paid by each young worker will go to his own ‘head’ in his own personal pension, calculated on the basis of the cumulative amount of contributions and remuneration. Every worker involved in this system will have, as I have said his own'multiple', where his contributions will be saved and invested in order to create a reserve with which future pensions will be paid. It was heard by colleagues who criticised this bill that social security is being privatised. Is that right, colleagues? Who will manage this fund? Any private corporations? It is provided for in the draft law, the Minister explained it, the creation of a public fund, the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, a legal person governed by public law, which will in fact operate by applying private-sector practice and investment philosophy. It will invest the savings, but mostly and sovereignly it will be subject to state control, state supervision. Ladies and gentlemen, it has also been said that we are gambling with the money of the young people insured. Do you really think that? Is it not ensured that anyway their invested capital will receive the money that they have already deposited, taking account of inflation? Is this gambling or making sure they get the pension on the basis of the contributions they have made? Moreover, insured persons are allowed to choose between three investment profiles, conservative, balanced and aggressive, and of course every five years, depending on their willingness to differentiate this investment profile so that they themselves are involved in the development of their insurance process. In conclusion, ladies and gentlemen, I would like to stress that this draft law is a flagrant draft, it is a forward plan and, above all, it looks at the future, looks at tomorrow's generation and ensures it. It is a reform that concerns young people and tries to reverse and reverse the belief of most young people that they will probably never retire. We are here, therefore, to ensure that they do not only receive a pension but they will also receive a higher pension and, above all, this will happen because we are legislating today for society, with a look at the future, with a look at tomorrow. We do not stick to the past and above all do not function in a party interest, but on the basis of the common good. I therefore support with all my powers this draft law, and I urge all of you to stand alongside the new generation, on the side of young people. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u139	Thank you, my colleague. Mr Lippe has the floor now from the New Democracy.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u140	Thank you, Madam President. Mr Minister, ladies and gentlemen, we are debating a bill which puts the supplementary insurance of the new generations on a new footing. A new fund, the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, is set up, which has the legal form of the legal person governed by public law. It is therefore clearly a question of a purely public fund without question. Where is the big change? For the first time private sector practices are introduced in its operation. The well-known distribution system is abandoned, which does not meet well under current social and economic conditions. The current proportion of retired workers is 1.7 to 1 with the situation getting worse and not improving. Thus, based on data from the National Accounting Authority in 2050, the average supplementary pension will be reduced from 16% to less than 10% of the average salary with the current system. The new ancillary insurance system is based on the capitalisation system of predefined contributions. It concerns all those who enter subsidiary insurance from 1-1-2022 and optional for those who are up to thirty-five years after they choose it. Every insured person has an individual account to which he will always have access - by electronic means - all the information concerning him, such as contributions paid or due, and the return on his investment. The insured person may, every three years, select an investment programme that he wishes or even a combination of programmes. The amount of the contributions remains until 2022 at 6.5% and then decreases to 6%. The new system offers many extra benefits as opposed to what is currently in place. If a person has not completed 15 years of insurance and wishes to do so, he will receive the actual value of his contributions back. Accordingly, there is care in the event of a death pension, even if no 15-year-old contributions had been paid, where the difference is covered by the State budget and the heir receives an supplementary pension. This is a thorough and costly system based on three studies to predict all its effects. Firstly, the proportionality, secondly, the macroeconomic and third, the sustainability of the public debt. I would particularly like to focus on the macroeconomic consequences of the new system, because they are those relating to the overall economy over time. There is a need, given the need to increase investment effort and build a saving culture. The new subsidiary security offers an additional opportunity for growth in the economy by creating savings and investing resources in the Greek economy. These elements, of course, will lead to an increase in GDP, a greater productivity and an increase in employment. The administration of the new fund shall be managed by persons of known prestige and high scientific training, with a five-year term in order not to depend on the life of the government concerned. The fund operates an investment committee with advisory powers in relation to the investment options of the EBA. It is important that the principle of prudent management and economic development is applied, while investments are made in the interests of those insured in the reminder that there should be no overreliance on a given product or undertaking. Once again, the opposition's counterpart is not based on figures and real facts. In the opposition's first argument, the answer is, no, today's bill does not affect the solidarity of generations, instead strengthens it. The current subsidiary insurance system has led young insured persons to feel that they are raising a disproportionately large burden, without being accounted for, for the payment of current pensions. In particular, young workers often devalue the value of paying their insurance contributions, often turning to black work. Secondly, it does not affect both the current and the future pensioners, since this is a system that offers security, stability and flexibility without affecting today's pensioners and workers, more than thirty-five years old. The supplementary insurance money is not the subject of a gamble, as we have heard from the SYRIZA Parliamentary Representative. The practice of investing in insurance contributions is already followed by Scandinavian countries with excellent results, and in any case there is a guarantee from the Greek State that the insured will receive the money, which has been paid in addition to inflation in the worst cases. In fact, based on OECD studies, the new subsidiary insurance system is expected to deliver higher pensions than 43% to 68%. Thirdly, it is not the taxpayers' burden at an unreasonable cost. The budgetary cost of transition to the new regime is estimated, as the Minister has heard, at EUR 120 million per year, and this is therefore a burden that the state budget can afford on an annual basis if we consider the benefits we receive. The Greek economy has benefited significantly over decades from investment in these funds and a significant increase in GDP and employment is expected. The question therefore is the overall positive development of the Greek economy. The Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs proceeds to a brave reform of subsidiary insurance in Greece by introducing attitudes that were unprecedented about Greek public data. It is a piece of legislation that deals fairly with all the insured, young and older and all pensioners, without any surprises. Unfortunately, in our country, we are used to running to correct the bad texts after having experienced the consequences. Today there is a decisive step forward in order to prevent developments in relation to supplementary insurance for the next generations. The demographic evolution of our country with Europe's second faster ageing population leaves us no room for reassurance. The new supplementary insurance system offers a sustainable solution to the new generations of people insured with higher supplementary pensions, while opening up new prospects in the Greek economy. Thank you.

Today's bill corrects a repeated injustice. It introduces a reformist incision into our insurance system. It forms a sustainable ancillary insurance model, a flexible arrangement that can be adjusted at regular intervals, a system that will account for and be governed by transparency and integrity rules. These changes in the supplementary insurance system allege the mistrust of our country's new human resources for where the money goes, which each month pays for insurance contributions. Having the opportunity to talk personally to young people and young people, who work on a wide range of professional categories either in the private sector, or as free professionals and self-employed, I find the feeling of vanity very intense. Most people feel that every month - just to say - they make money out of the wallet by removing it from their income without expecting any reward. Today this changes definitively. Additional insurance, away from dangerous experiments, turns home and benefits our young people without our current pensioners being at risk. With effect from 1 January 2022 each new worker will finance his own pension. His monthly contributions will be saved in an individual piggyback. The worker will know and have control at all times of his supplementary pension, which will ultimately be higher than the current model. This is evidenced by the international successful implementation of this system, which can lead to an increase in the order of 43% to 68%. This is because today's monthly contributions to supplementary insurance will not be saved simply, but will be reinvested by a multi-speed system, either in a more conservative way or in a much more aggressive manner, depending on the choice of each insured person. And because the Civic Opposition, addicted to misinformation and dispersal of false news, dare not be written with the self-evident solution that will put our insurance system on tracks of irreversible sustainability, once again selects cheap and obsolete slogan. SYRIZA's ladies and gentlemen, subsidiary insurance is not only privatised, but the contributions of young workers are now fully guaranteed up to the last euro and are guaranteed both by the distortions of the current system and by potential shocks of the global economy and its temporary crisis. All the insured persons, which will be subject to the new capitalising subsidiary insurance model, will receive, irrespective of the performance of their returns, the entire amount paid even if the yield has a negative mark. And all this, without the present pensions being at least risk. So stop the terrorist campaign by fishing in blurred waters. This recipe has failed miserably. And one more thing I deserve to point out. The amendment, which has been tabled today, provides for the extension of the reduction in insurance contributions to employers and workers by three percentage points up to 31 December 2022, which means, in practice, that wage costs are reduced from the maintenance of reduced contributions by the end and next year. Ladies and gentlemen, the Government of New Democracy continues its reform work throughout the country's social and economic life at the same time. The new supplementary insurance system opens the door wide opens to the future of young workers, who will now feel safe, confident and optimistic that what they pay today will be returned to them in full, if not increased, in the future. It is a new reform with a long-term perspective that the Government of the New Democracy and Mr Mitsotakis will be applying for the first time in our country, which, with carefully and studied steps, ensures in our new generation, in our afflicted new generation, which has grown in the crisis and insecurity, what is really entitled. Remove from insurance its national and social character. You turn it into a compulsory for each individual employee, individual participation in the games of the financial markets. That is why even in the title of the draft law, you yourself said: ‘Insert a capitalisation system for predefined contributions to subsidiary insurance’. You say supplementary and not social security. In fact, here, the state is not just an ombudsman or a mediator. It works as a normal propulsion, forcing all workers to pay their working life contributions to the hands of private businesses, passports. So you are making all the popular and social bases mandatory, all the workers of the next generations, customers of the most parasitic part of society and the economy, the same who has created all the last global economic crises. One could also speak of relationships between dependency and mandate. But that is not the case. You are also making a drastic reduction in the State's obligations towards citizens and against workers. The responsible state body will simply collect the contributions and give them to the hands of the 'harks' of the financial markets and, if possible, without any effective control over their management. Neither will the state control management nor the insured. To date, in subsidiary insurance, the insured were able to control their participation in the administrative bodies of their insurance institution, and I mean the board of directors, but also the general assembly. You with characteristic liberal comfort send all this to the waste bin. There is no substantial provision in your bill to control the management of these huge funds. From this law there is a basic paragraph missing, the phrase imposed by law in all these cases ‘that insurance contributions are not guaranteed and previous returns do not guarantee future returns’. But you may also be right about the omission. This is necessary to protect the citizen, who voluntarily comes and chooses investments of this kind. But here, the worker has no choice. You're forcing him to deposit his contributions there. In 2008, with the collapse of the financial markets, they were lost by the individual-account capitalist systems - listen to number, fellow Members - 5.4 trillion dollars and, for example, say they had losses in Ireland 38%, in Australia 27% and in the United States 26%. And of course, you are overlooking it and you are predicting a state guarantee. So what does the state guarantee the insured, ladies and gentlemen? Only the return on contributions plus inflation. No one, of course, can guarantee that there will be no financial crisis throughout the whole year of worker insurance. To what, then, can every person insured with his pension hope? Only in periods of over-performance of the financial markets. This communal bait is projected by this bill. But we all know that these are very short periods of time and they are almost always the willingness of a new crisis. In the meantime, you are making progress in this system with full knowledge that it will bear a huge burden on the state budget, the famous cost of transition. Costs that will again be borne by taxpayers. It is therefore an additional cost to the insured. Today you separate the insured in two castes, the old and young people entering from 1-1-2022 and then, with two independent insurance systems. Youth contributions will not be used to pay the pensions of the old, nor the old and early retirement insurance funds for young people. Others estimate the cost of transition to EUR 56 billion. All studies show it from 49 billion to 78 billion. We all hope that they will not be disproved, as is usually the case, and that we do not have even greater swelling. ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u203	Thank you, Mr. Karathanasopou. Mr. Ioannis Melas has the floor, and then Mrs. Anastasia Garas.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u204	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, we are debating a bill today on the future of the insurance of thousands of workers. The Law of the Ministry of Labour on the Reform of Subsidy concerns mainly the next generations and new workers. This insurance reform aims to gradually transform the ancillary insurance system from a redistributive redistributive today into a capitalisation. With the new system individual buttons are created, we could say, of which the future pensions of young workers will be paid. What's going on today, though? At present, workers' contributions are used to pay the pensions of the current pensioners. The question raised to every new worker is: Why should I pay? Am I going to retire? The Minister said so before. What's going to happen from now on? Contributions will be saved and invested by creating a reserve from which their future pensions will be paid. The contributions of each new worker will go to his own pension. For this purpose, therefore, the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, which will be a legal entity governed by public law and invest the funds from the contributions, is created. In other words, with this new system we give the new insured person the opportunity to choose from a small number of investment profiles to invest the funds of his insurance portfolio. At regular intervals, in fact, it will be possible to change this profile, having full control of its contributions and their returns. Its supplementary pension will be based on all their contributions and remuneration. The new supplementary insurance system will therefore be compulsory for employees and self-employed persons who enter the labour market for the first time from 1-1 to 2022, optional for persons under thirty-five years who either do not have subsidiary insurance or who are already insured in the existing system, and the level of contributions for the new subsidiary system remains unchanged. Ladies and gentlemen, the changes in the functioning of social security always create tensions and disagreements between the parties. However, it is important to have a real dialogue with arguments. Unfortunately, in today's debate the arguments from Opposition are missing. Opposition disagrees with this insurance reform and accuses us of privatising social security, of reducing current pensions and that the cost of transition is going to be high. But is all this true? Obviously, it's not. Social security is not privatised and remains public. The Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund set up will operate under the supervision of the Greek State, while the guarantee is provided that the new insured will not receive a supplementary pension lower than that corresponding to the contributions they have paid. There is no reduction in existing pensions. It is provided for in the bill - Article 59 - that existing pensions are not reduced. The pensions of the insured persons to be retired under the current scheme will continue to be calculated on the basis of the existing rules and will not be affected. Also, the cost of transition is not high, as the opposition is trying to present. According to IOBE, the National Analogue Authority and DPRKH, it amounts to EUR 120 million per year, while the state today - the Minister said so earlier - has 15 billion a year for the payment of pensions. Ladies and gentlemen, this reform is necessary because the demographic problem is growing and it takes time to deal with it. Already the Government of New Democracy has taken important initiatives to deal with it. But we have to keep things quiet so we don't find ourselves in the future with our backs on the wall. The current distribution system only works effectively when many workers finance the pensions of a few pensioners. But that no longer applies to our country. In order to understand the problem, in the first decades of operation of the social security system in Greece four or five employees corresponded to each pensioner. Today they account for only 1.7 employees in every pensioner and the prospects are not at all positive. Specifically for subsidiary insurance according to the National Accounting Authority, the proportion of persons insured from 2,6 in 2020 will decline to 1,7 in 2050, i.e. 2050 1,9 million pensioners will share the contributions of 3,2 million insured. So what do we want with this reform? Prevent the consequences of the demographic problem in social security, create savings of part of which will be invested in the Greek economy by strengthening growth and the creation of better paid jobs, give higher pensions to the insured of the new system. For example, a worker who is paid a minimum wage of EUR 650 and with forty years of insurance today receives an supplementary pension at EUR 153. With the new scheme, pensions can be raised to EUR 219. To increase, i.e. by 43% with returns equal to the average of the OECD member-state capitalisation pension schemes. It may even be increased to EUR 257 if returns are achieved equal to the average annual return on the reserves of the ECA which manages the gross mutual capital of the ‘ASAFLISTIC ORGANISATIONS’. We are trying to strengthen the sustainability of our insurance system and limit undeclared work. Ladies and gentlemen, this reform concerns the new generations and the future of our country. It responds to international developments, as societies and economies are transformed. It forms a modern, innovative and ambitious social security system. It is not addressed either to the right or to the left or to parties alone. It also concerns young workers who must stay in our country and work and move forward. We are shaping and promoting the insurance future of the new generation. Without young people there can be neither progress nor progress nor development. In recent years, indeed, the phenomenon of Brain Drain, where thousands of young people have left for abroad, has caused enormous problems in our country. This section of the supplementary insurance system is a small one, so that we do not have to be again confronted with a new Brain Drain. This system ensures that our young people will work and be able to control the supplementary pension they receive themselves. In this reform, then, the past is being mixed with the future. In conclusion, Mr President, I should like to refer to topical and urgent subjects of major importance. ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u221	I had announced Mr. Vesyropoulos, but Mr. Tsakloglou will speak in the capacity of the Deputy Minister for Social Affairs and Mr. Vesyropoulos will talk about the amendment a short time later. Mr. Undersecretary, you have the floor.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u222	Thank you very much, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, following a close study and one-year work on a legal, proportional and economic level, I have the honour of proposing, of course, with the Minister, Mr Hatzidakis, the draft law on subsidiary insurance reform. The draft law is a governmental prognostic initiative based on good practices that have been in place for decades by European states with a enviable welfare state. But you will allow me to begin my position with a historical regression: eighty-nine years ago in this Chamber the first systematic attempt to introduce social security into our country was being discussed. Mutual funds were already in operation, but insurance was exhausted in the same business without universal coverage of employees and later, as expected, of other occupational categories. The legislative initiative was prepared by the Venizelos Government, which had received a high level of technical assistance from external experts, mainly from the International Labour Office, which was reflected in the draft's truly excellent report. The bill, however, was passed by the Papanastassiou government, which had parliamentary support for liberals. During his speech from this Step, Eleftherios Venizelos stressed that "By social security, the Greek society will be better trained and equipped to cope with all the risks that society is facing today". At that time, in the depths of the economic crisis launched by the United States in 1929, an interesting public debate was taking place in our country in the area of radical liberal center, with perhaps George Theotokas, who was trying to identify policies to create growth and prosperity for all, namely what we now call 'inclusive growth', this 'inclusive growth', as is said in English. The social security bill was part of this logic. Similar questions arise at the current juncture. Other crises in both our country and internationally bring to the heart of the debate the issue of inclusive development, especially in its intergenerational dimension. In all respects - economic, climate and so on - we are burdening the new generation with a great deal of overweight. This trend cannot and must not continue. The draft 'Security reform for the new generation', such as the title of the bill, is precisely part of this logic. It changes the architecture of supplementary insurance, so that the country's insurance system can be properly adapted to address the serious challenges that the demographic ageing of the population and other diseases such as the size of undeclared work and thus the Greek economy's development model. And, as in the respective Venizelos - Papanastassiou bill, this bill has become an important advance, which is reflected, among other things, in the three accompanying studies, which are proportional, macroeconomic and sustainability of public debt prepared respectively by the National Analogue Authority, IOBE and DPRKH. Ladies and gentlemen, the Greek society is growing and growing rapidly. The fertility rate, i.e. the average number of children born by women of childbearing age, is now 1.4 and is well below the number that would enable the population to be stabilised, which is 2.1. At the same time, due to progress in health and hygiene, the expectation of survival has been steadily increasing for many decades. These developments lead to a rapid increase in the ratio of dependency, i.e. the number of older people to the working age or the ratio of retired workers. The existing social security system is almost exclusively distributed, i.e. the contributions of current workers pay the pensions of current pensioners. The distribution systems work satisfactorily when the workers are many and the pensioners are few, that is, the ratio of dependence is low. But they are experiencing considerable difficulties when this relationship is inverted. In the first decades of operation of the social security system in Greece, the ratio was four to five employees for each pensioner. Today the corresponding figure is only 1.7 and all the studies we have show us that the prospects are nothing but roses. Demographic ageing is not exclusively a Greek phenomenon. It is seen in almost all developed countries and gradually in more and more developing countries. In most of the other developing countries, when they perceived the importance of demographic risk for their distribution pension system, they introduced or strengthen the capitalisation pillar of the system, precisely in order to achieve a differentiation of insurance risk and reduce the system's exposure to demographic risk. Today in countries such as Denmark, the Netherlands or Sweden - to mention just a few countries with an enviable level of social protection - the vast majority of workers have a supplementary capitalist insurance system. In many countries the reserves of the pension funds exceed 100% of GDP and in some even 200% of GDP. In almost all OECD countries, the capital funds have had a great deal of growth in recent decades. This attempts to do this in our country, admittedly decades behind, this bill. What exactly does the bill provide for? The draft law provides for the gradual transformation of subsidiary insurance from distribution to capitalisation. The reform only concerns subsidiary insurance and new workers. The main pension remains as it is. In particular, for new entrants on the labour market from 1st of the new year with compulsory subsidiary insurance and optional for those who are insured under thirty-five years with or without the subsidiary insurance obligation to choose the scheme, subsidiary insurance contributions shall be directed to individual accounts, invested on the basis of their preferences from the fund to be established, the Capital Insurance Fund, the EEKA, and their pension shall be determined by all their contributions and returns on their investments. The TECA is a legal person governed by public law. The selection of the Management Board and the Steering Board shall be carried out by particularly strict meriting procedures and always under the auspices of the AACC. Its function is based on professional management. Good governance standards are adopted and there is transparency in both its functions and the information provided. Each insured person will have full information on the contributions and returns of his investment in real time from his or her computer or cell phone. Two guarantees are provided in the bill: the first to the members of the existing system that their pensions will continue to be calculated on the basis of the current rules and there will be no pension cut. The second to the members of the new system that the pension they receive will at least correspond to the amount of contributions they have paid in real terms, i.e. taking into account the effect of inflation. In other words, the State guarantees non-negative returns. In other words, only returns on investment are actually subject to market fluctuations and not to the contributions paid. However, I must say that in the light of the experience of many other countries and also of the simulations we have made, we will need to use this guarantee in a very few cases. In addition, there are two favourable differences in the draft law in relation to the existing system. First, insurers who do not complete 15 years of insurance are reimbursed in real terms, but not in return for their investments. Secondly, in the case of invalidity or death of an insured person with a low contribution, a pension equal to a minimum of 15 years of unskilled worker's contributions is provided. Ladies and gentlemen, this reform has four objectives: Firstly, to reduce the exposure of social security to demographic risk, to achieve a differentiation of the insurance risk to the whole social security and thus to strengthen the stability of the social security system as a whole. The members of the new system will receive three pensions: national, reward and subsidiary. These are subject to different risks: budgetary, demographic, markets. However, given that these risks do not have a very high positive correlation, the overall risk is decreasing and this improves the stability of the entire social security system. In other words, we don't put all our eggs in a basket. And not to forget that social security is first of all a form of insurance and that is a basic principle of insurance. [[At this point, the closing bell of the speaking time of the Secretary-General is ringing.]] Mr President, I had the feeling that it was fifteen minutes at my disposal.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u223	You can also use your dentistry.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u224	Secondly, create savings, an important part of which will be invested in the Greek economy by boosting productivity and economic growth, creating more and better paid jobs, leading to higher direct and indirect taxes and social security contributions. Let us not forget that investment is basically the fuel of growth and the proportion of investment in GDP in Greece is the lowest in the European Union. Thirdly, to pay higher pensions to insured persons in the new system. The existing supplementary insurance system is what we call capitalisation and a zero deficit concept. In practice this means that every year all the pensions provided must be equal to the total insurance contributions. Consequently, the rate of replacement of supplementary pensions, i.e. the ratio of the average supplementary pension to the average salary, depends on the development of the ratio between the number of pensioners and the number of persons insured. This is worsening over time, and this leads to a significant reduction in the rate of replacement. According to the estimates of the National Analogue Authority, from 16% that is currently the rate of the supplementary pension, it will fall to 9.5% in just three decades. As I said before, we do not discover the wheel. Such capitalisation pension schemes have been operating for decades in many countries. The experience of these countries shows that the real performances of the capitalist systems - as Mr Hatzidakis has already shown - have higher variations than those of the distribution systems, but in the long term they are much higher and especially those of the old-age distribution systems such as the Greek one. Based on this experience, we believe that the pensions of the new system will be considerably higher than those of the existing system. Fourthly, to restore the confidence of the new generation in the insurance system. The creation of individual kumparads creates strong counter-incentives for youth participation in uninsured work. This helps restore youth confidence in the insurance system by linking their contributions in a tangible and transparent way to pension and by responding to the concern expressed by many young people that "I will never retire". At the same time, the reduction of uninsured work will have an obvious positive impact on both the incomes of the insurance system and the Greek economy in general. Several counter-problems have been heard, in my opinion, most of them have been completely unfounded, which I have already answered in the debate in the Committee on Social Affairs and I shall also refer to my second language. But I would like here to stand up to the only counter-protect that has been heard and could be well founded, but it is not. It is that of the transfer cost. The cost of transition is precisely because no reductions are to be made in the pensions of the existing system. Depending on the discount rate, the National Accounting Authority calculated that this cost is from something less than EUR 50 billion to more than EUR 70 billion. In the central scenario with discounted prices, the transition costs are estimated at EUR 56 billion. I repeat, however, that we are talking about a 50-year-old depth. To understand the comparison measure, last year alone for the support of the pension system, the budget gave EUR 16 billion, i.e. the corresponding cost will be covered in just four years - only four years - budget grants. But that is not the main argument. The main argument is that this is the gross cost. Why is it the gross cost? I said before that that a significant part of the resources of the new fund are to be invested in the Greek economy. That is what boosts economic growth and that leads to higher taxes and social security contributions. According to the IOBE's macroeconomic study, in the central scenario the additional financial benefits are about EUR 50 billion. Beyond the fifty-year period - which is the timeframe of the economic studies submitted to Parliament - the benefits surpass with a very large margin any cost, i.e. after this fifty-year period, the benefits are clearly much higher than any cost which is too small at that stage. Therefore, the net cost of transition is fully managed and this is beyond the other benefits for the workers and the Greek economy. However, I would like to point out, by addressing the future finance and labour ministers, that while the gross cost of transition will be recorded in the budget, the benefits will be distributed to the economy and the additional budget revenues generated will not be recorded in the budget in a discreet manner. This, of course, does not mean that they do not exist at all. After all, let us not forget, as several speakers have said, that an important part of the development of the Greek economy in the past has actually been financed by the surpluses of social security in the early years of operation of the system and, obviously, this has had a very large financial impact as well. Ladies and gentlemen, during the ten years of economic crisis before we followed many barren controversy, while the country was at the immediate risk of bankruptcy. In particular, as far as insurance is concerned, the controversies were very strong. The result, however, was common and unfavourable to anyone who came to the government and faced reality. Opposition usually focuses on what costs it has an insurance reform. This is relatively easy to measure. What costs, however, do we have when our economy is stuck in low growth because of the non-implementation of the reform? That is the really important question, and that is what we need to answer. If something should have been learnt from the ten-year crisis, it is the value of consensus and fruitful counter-population, of counter-population that is aware of the problems and proposes solutions and improvements. Insurance is a national issue and, as in foreign policy, there is a little - quite a national line and a culture of cross-party consultation, so should insurance. This is something - some of the speakers have pointed out earlier - which is already happening in some countries. Insurance is not about a government's term of office, but it is about many governments, because, for what a government decides inevitably, the next governments will also be involved. The Additional Insurance Reform Act is a significant long-term structural reform with the main beneficiaries of the new generation that will enjoy higher pensions in the future, without prejudice to the living standards of the pensioners of the existing system. It has a proactive character and strengthens the stability of the entire social security system, strengthens the country's development prospects and helps both to limit undeclared work and restore the confidence of young people in social security. This is a major step towards modernising the social security system that comes - to use the words of Freedom Venizelos - to face a major risk facing society today. It would be a good thing if this bill were supported by wider powers within Parliament's Chamber. We really owe it to our children and grandchildren. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u234	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the government continues to act with lasting institutional intervention in order to facilitate the return to the normality of all citizens, but also to support those who need it. The procedure for these institutional interventions does not have static features, but has a dynamic effect. We are fully aware of the reality, the difficulties faced by citizens and especially those affected by either pandemic or natural disasters. Amendment No 1045 is also in line with this and with a special number of 71 tabled by the Ministry of Finance by the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs, which is currently being discussed in plenary. This amendment contains two specific articles. The first article is linked to the discharge from HNPH and for the year 2021 of the real estate located in the areas of the Attica Region and the Corinthian Regional Unit affected by the fires on 23 and 24 July of 2018. For the years 2018, 2019 and 2020, it is known that an exemption has been granted for the years 2018, 2019 and 2020. The government, however, considered that the conditions imposed by these people's support have not been differentiated, that the consequences for the citizens who saw their properties have not been completely compromised. That is why we have proceeded with the extension of the HNVO discharge for the firefighting citizens of the regions concerned and for the year 2021. It is a substantial measure of support and tax relief. The second article of the amendment is linked to the extension of the deadline for the submission of tax declarations, which was established so that all citizens can be facilitated and consistent with their obligations. The provisions contained in Article 2 of the Amendment are hereby extended until 10 September to include the deadline for the submission of tax returns for tax year 2020 for natural persons and legal persons or legal entities referred to in Article 45 of the Income Tax Code, whose tax year expires on 31 December 2020. At the same time, the timeframe for payment of the income tax is extended. In particular, the tax will be paid in eight monthly instalments, the first two instalments being paid by 17 September. From there on, with regard to members of companies with simplified books, including, among other things, partners in joint ventures, public limited companies or urban companies, they have the opportunity to deposit their tax declaration by 15 September and to pay the first instalment by 30 September 2021. These are regulations aimed at facilitating citizens and businesses. In the same direction, the institutional intervention carried out by the Government last July for the payment of HICP in six instalments was initiated, with the first being paid by the end of September. Ladies and gentlemen, I believe that the content of the amendment is a field of understanding and agreement between all the political forces and that can also be expressed in its vote. As I mentioned at the beginning of my speech, the government is constantly updating its institutional interventions with measures aimed at supporting citizens and returning to normality. Thank you.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u238	First of all, good month from me. Mr President, Minister, ladies and gentlemen, the need to improve the supplementary insurance system is not a reform, for which we must oppose or poison public political speech. It is a reform that needs to be carried out, in order to have a sustainable system, a supplementary insurance system for the next generations. This reform comes as a follow-up to the Brutsi Act on Main Pensions, of Law 4670, which was a real incision, as it dissociated income from the amount of contributions, giving freedom and option of choosing the insurance grade. We went through a new era and all the people of the market confess it, the free traders, businessmen, the people of society who pay their insurance contributions. It was high time, then, that we created a more modern system of insurance, a system that meets the modern requirements and needs of society and of workers. The existing distribution system could no longer work. The reason is simple. The Minister mentioned it earlier. The proportion of pensioners to workers has changed radically. In the 1960s, the ratio was one to six, and today the ratio is a pensioner to 1,7 employees. No matter how much a worker pays, he cannot meet the pensioner's pension requirements. In the face of this need, we are proposing the solution of the individual pigeon, the main element of the insurance reform, a system which is implemented in many modern European countries, which they have created before being faced with such problems. With the individual money there will be greater control over the hands of young insured persons, the system will become a capitalisation, a reform which will result in significantly higher pensions for young insured persons up to 68%, and it is certain that old insured and retired people are not affected. Some elements, which were also mentioned by the Minister and by other colleagues, but I think it is important that they should be repeated once again to reflect the arguments of gambling, the invasion of individuals, neoliberal practices, and I shall mention again. The reserve, i.e. the funds of the new insured, will manage the new state Treasury under public law under the short name TECA, as we have said. There will also be three investment profiles among which he will choose insured: the preservative, the balanced and the aggressive one, and the possibility of a five-year change. This system applies to advanced countries such as Sweden, Denmark, the Netherlands and has been shown to lead to higher supplementary pensions from 43% to 68%. I was working in such a system, Minister, and that was the insurance policy. So there will be a double safety net. The first will concern young people, with the guarantee of the public of non-negative performance. The second will concern the payment of supplementary pensions for old insured persons with an explicit guarantee in the new law. The proportional study has included all demographic forecasts. The total cost of transition is estimated at 120 million. It is true that, somewhere in history, insight and genius were divorced to the Greeks and we are forced today to rediscover the wheel from the beginning. When the current system of insurance was established, the so-called 'distributive' about six decades ago, the proportion of retired workers was 4 to 1. And this ratio has been estimated to be the minimum required for the insurance and pensions to be viable. But today, given the serious demographic problem, which concerns not only our country, but much of the developed countries - the problem is very likely to be reversible - we understand that we cannot continue on the same path, we cannot continue on the same logic. The ministers said the ratio today is 1.7 to 1. For almost a year, twenty thousand new applications have been put into the system for pension applications every month.
