At the same time, there are many people who are wrong to believe that they are paying contributions for their own pension, so it is also guaranteed. Of course, this is not the case, as in the distribution system implemented in our country, youth contributions are used to pay the current pensions. The bill brought by the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs that we are discussing today comes to restore the injustice suffered by the new generation of workers and we must say it because we must tell the Greek people the truth, while at the same time this bill is a real, important and necessary reform of our insurance system. And just as his title is, it's a bill exclusively focused on the new generation of our country. But what changes, ladies and gentlemen, with today's bill? With a delay of many years, our country introduces for supplementary pensions exclusively for workers entering the labour market and optionally for workers up to thirty-five years a capitalist system, as many European countries have been applying for years, such as the Netherlands, like Sweden. Is this really what it means and how it will be implemented? By means of an individual pigment, the insurance contributions paid by the young worker himself are paid for the payment of his own supplementary pension. In addition, this money is invested. Yeah, it's not bad to invest the money. It's not a criminal offense to invest the money, of course. And as we will see below, the worker has choices in terms of investment programmes. He chooses investment projects himself. They are invested at the same time - look out - they are strengthening the Greek economy and we must not forget that. To this end, the Capital Insurance Fund is established, the so-called TEA, which we will hear from now on very often. The TECA is a legal person governed by public law. It is supervised by the Ministry of Labour, the National Accounting Authority, as well as by the Securities Committee and its operation starts in a few months, on January 2022. The administrative bodies, the selection process, staffing, responsibilities, responsibilities, penalties for members of the administrative bodies, the operating specifications are clearly described in the individual articles of this draft law and ensure the high quality of the organisation and functioning of the new fund. For example, in Article 12 - because we need to speak specifically in this Chamber - the procedure for selecting the board of directors is done by an open public procedure. The members shall be chosen by an independent committee adviser. A similar procedure shall be for the CEO as provided for in Article 24. It is very important that Article 8, which refers to the Fund's resources and emphasises that the grant from the State budget, in terms of its operating expenditure, has a transitional effect and is foreseen for a maximum of five years. Articles 17 and 18 define sanctions and the cessation of members of the Board of Directors, and Article 19 states that the members of the Board of Directors shall be appointed. they have criminal and civil liability for acts or omissions thereof, or if they have acted with deceit or serious negligence. Very important are the provisions of the third part, such as Articles 43 and 44, which regulate the issues of contributions, while the existing mechanism of e-ECA is defined as the existing mechanism of e-ECA, so that there is no additional cost to create a separate mechanism. [[At this point the end of the speaking time of the honourable Member is ringing at the end of the bell.]] Mr President, if I can have your tolerance for a minute. Articles 47 and 48 concern the investment strategy and investment programmes, and Article 62 below describes the possibility of selecting a programme or a combination of programmes. Article 53 ensures that the insured person does not lose his contributions, the contributions he has paid, but will be reimbursed in the event of the non-fulfilment of fifteen years of insurance. Under Article 60, the State guarantees a minimum monthly supplementary pension, which may not be less than the contributions paid. Finally, Article 64 provides the possibility of creating a pension right through the redemption of the remaining time and to those who have completed at least twelve years of insurance, so that no one will lose the supplementary pension. In conclusion, because of today's opposition bill, words like "joy", "hard", "destruction" and more have been heard, I would like to point out that none of this really responds to the reality! ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u272	Thank you, Mr President. Minister, ladies and gentlemen, with the bill we are debating today, let me use the expression, the vehicle of the capitalisation system in subsidiary insurance begins. Who will travelers be? Travellers in this vehicle on the basis of the provisions of the law will be new entrants from 1-1 to 2022 in the labour market, who are, of course, liable for subsidiary insurance and voluntary workers up to thirty-five years, as well as self-employed. According to the explanatory memorandum to the draft law, this system seeks firstly to ensure, with the main basic pension of a standard of living as equivalent to what the worker would have had if he were working and had his own earnings. This is therefore very important because the needs continue to exist in households in the country. The second thing that the draft law is trying to cover is that we must finally - we have heard it beforehand - strengthen the sustainability of the social security system. The capitalist system - because this is what we are talking about - does not affect the fundamental characteristics of subsidiary insurance, which retains its public character and is under state control, on the basis of what is provided for in the Constitution. Now, instead of being used to pay the supplementary pension for the current pensioners, you will be saved and every worker will now have one of his own money - we have heard it earlier - where the contributions and returns from his capital will be collected there, which will be paid when the time comes for his future pension. So what does Greece do? It does the simple thing, it follows the example of states that have already been considered a success. For example, in the OECD, we know that 50% and more of the insured are based on supplementary pensions in a capitalised system, and in other countries this figure is 80%. As the committee has now referred to failure examples of some countries by opposition MPs, I stress that a single part of the overall social security system is being taken over in this draft law and not all, as happened in Latin American states, as well as in Eastern Bloc states where the system collapsed and workers' earnings collapsed. Will anyone ask: is reform necessary? The answer is certainly yes, it is necessary, for the following reasons. The first reason is the country's demographic problem. I would remind you that when the distribution system was founded in 1950, the working-to-retirement ratio was 4 to 1, and already on the basis of what the Deputy Minister, Mr Tsakloglu, told us, is 1 to 1,7 and can therefore be said at some point on the basis of the forecasts it can reach 1 to 2. That is to say, in our country, they work fewer and support most. As you can see, this cannot go on like this. This draft law attempts in practice a mixture which reduces the risk that the pension scheme may entail. What does that mean? Firstly, the national pension is subject to the financial risk - I do not need to develop it - the return pension is subject to a demographic risk - I said two words earlier - and the capitalisation pension, the subsidiary we are talking about, is subject to the risk of the markets. So we have a mixture of dangers. Through this mixture, we will certainly have an expanded benefit base that we will try to make sure that it is stable. A second element is that the pensioners of the new system will get much higher pensions than those of the existing system, because they have much higher long-term yields, which will of course take thirty or forty years. Thirdly, restoring the confidence of the new generation in the insurance system, since, as we have heard earlier, each worker will have his own individual account. Of course, all this reform is based on three studies: the proportionality, the budgetary and the sustainability study of the public debt. That gives a safety to design. But we have also heard criticism of the opposition. But what is this criticism? The first point of criticism is that it speaks of privatization of social security. The answer is that this is completely false. Why? Because Article 4 provides for the establishment of the EAEC, the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, which will be in the form of a legal person and managed by a board and the Director, based on the provisions of the draft law, which cannot be called into question in terms of their objectivity. The second point of criticism is what Anti-Policy says will increase the level of contributions. And that's false. It's not true, let me say it more politely. For the new subsidiary system, the level of contributions remains the same, i.e. 6.5% by mid-2022 and 6% below for the employees on the basis of the insurance category they choose, but also for the self-employed. The third point of criticism: Opposition speaks of gambling with the money of the insured. That's completely false, because there's security valves. We have heard that the first challenge is that young people insured have the public guarantee in terms of the amount of benefits, in other words, where - and this seems unlikely - the Greek public will be in a position to enjoy both the contributions paid and the sums resulting from inflation. The second key concerns the current pensioners, and this lies in the commitment that neither the payment of supplementary pensions of the existing system, i.e. pensioners, will be affected by the planned reform. We therefore know that, because any gap exists will be covered by the public investment programme, by the state budget. With regard to the fourth point of criticism, the opposition is talking about a huge transition cost. It is well known, we have heard it, it has been analysed that the cost of transition, at the moment the fixed cost, is about EUR 15 billion and will be charged around EUR 130 million, a relatively small sum, I would say, in relation to the amount already planned and spent. Ladies and gentlemen, I say that reform of the supplementary insurance system will lead to - and this is what has been seen and is one of the examples of other countries - higher supplementary pensions for young insured persons, without any doubt - and I said earlier on - an impact on the old insured person, guarantees that the amount of the pension cannot be lower than that which corresponds to the contributions paid, through the State guarantee, and this is clearly provided for in the draft law, disconnects supplementary pensions from demographic developments and the progressive ageing of the population, responds to the high dependency of the state budget - we have said so earlier - and strengthens the confidence of the young people in this new public insurance system, as every new worker will be able to see the money of his money and can still plan after five years and change of the insurance level, strengthens the sustainability of the social security system and finally follows the fallen, which has been done, but a lot of which has been achieved by this system, and has not been brought to bear fruit.

ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u283	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, we are discussing today an important reform of our insurance system, capitalising subsidiary insurance, that is to say, a system in which the employees' contributions support the supplementary pension of the pensioners in a system where the supplementary pension is paid out by the savings of each insured person, i.e. the contributions paid in the years worked plus their return on investment. It is important, ladies and gentlemen, to say why this is happening and what benefits it will have for our insurance system as a whole, but also for future pensioners. First of all, the stability of the social security system is strengthened because, with the current system, the redistributive, supplementary pensions, like the main ones, will more and more face demographic pressure in the future, namely that workers will be called upon to support more and more pensioners. With their passage to the capitalisation system, supplementary pensions cease to be exposed to the same risk as the main ones, the demographic one. We share eggs in different baskets, as the Ministers have rightly said. Apart from that with capitalisation, we have a greater return on contributions, thereby encouraging tax evasion to be reduced, savings increase something that leads to an increase in investment in our economy, an increase in income and employment productivity and a total increase in GDP. A responsible political leadership must face up to the challenges of the future by giving solutions now not leaving the situation as it is nor by postponing the solution for later. Unfortunately, the Opposition faced a piece of legislation that is coming to improve the prospect of insurance in terms of ideological automation, as was done in the recent working draft, with arguments, the same ones that said about insurance for for forty and fifty years ago, with views that have little or nothing to do with the provisions of the bill and which are therefore being demolished by the evidence. Contrary to what you support, supplementary pensions with the capitalisation system will be higher, 43% to 68% higher on the basis of European experience, 11% to 13% in line with the national Analogue Authority's basic scenario. In other words, it replaces a system which, due to demographic pressure, would give smaller pensions to a system with the same contributions. Contrary to what you say, this is not a gamble for the contributions of the young insured. Because even in the unlikely event that something is wrong with the investments in their savings, the state guarantees that they will receive an additional pension equal to the contributions they would have paid plus inflation. So where is gambling when it is ensured that the insured will get at the worst the actual value of what he gave, or when the state completes the required contributions, so that supplementary pension can be granted in the event of death or disability? Does they consider the investment of the reserves of the insurance funds on the Greek market, as is done under the responsibility of two public bodies which have been operating for some twenty years, with a real return close to and above the average of the corresponding investments in the most developed countries in the world? So where is the party of the funds and insurance companies? On the contrary, the social security system is not privatised. The Auxiliary Social Security Fund, which will manage the reserves, is a legal person governed by public law and as such exercises public authority in order to achieve a specific public interest. In other words, social security remains under state care, as required by the Constitution. It was also argued that eighteen countries left the capitalist system in recent years and that this was a confession of its failure. They forget to tell us that these are countries of South America, Africa and Eastern Europe that have fully privatised their insurance systems, and that the model of capitalisation has been fully implemented, and in our case capitalisation will be a very small part of the insurance and initially a certain part of the insured and secondly, the successful Nordic model of public compulsory insurance is being used. Contrary to what you are saying, it does not result in an unfavourable financial cost for us to move from one system to another. The net cost is only 6 billion euros that are shared in about fifty years. In other words, a EUR 120 million a year - when the state gives more than EUR 15 billion for pensions every year - so that young workers can receive a much greater supplementary pension than the current system. In addition, all the scenarios examined by the ancillary insurance reform do not adversely affect public debt. On the contrary, it will lead to a lower ratio of debt to GDP over time. Ladies and gentlemen, against the ghosts seen by the Opposition, the new subsidiary insurance serves the social and economic objectives of a pension system, supports the living level of pensioners with higher benefits, increases the remuneration of the contributions and guarantees minimum remuneration, strengthens the confidence of younger workers in the public insurance system, facilitates future pensioners to plan their finances after the completion of their working life, mobilises savings with positive imprint on new domestic investments and employment, and reduces the overall risk to the insurance system. In short, it is a reform with a look at the future, with a look at the new generation combining social solidarity with economic development. Thank you.

The pandemic, the protracted recession of over the past few years, geopolitical developments with side effects for our country, the climate crisis is conditions which each individual would find difficult to cope with, especially when all of this happens at the same time or for succession. To some extent, we can say that this is why we are talking today, ladies and gentlemen, about returning hope to young people and young people who are planning the future, accepting that they may never receive a pension, as many of my fellow citizens have told me in Pella. The draft law proposed today by the Ministry of Labour and Social Affairs is a due reform for young people. The main objective of the draft law is to increase supplementary pensions in a timely manner. A fundamental reform in this direction is the introduction of the much-discussed capitalisation system into the national insurance system. This is a debate which I have been remembering for about twenty years both at political level in the House and outside, as well as at scientific and academic level. Unfortunately, we are facing an unpleasant situation. The population of the country is ageing. The rapid decline in births in combination with an increase in life expectancy results in a pensioner corresponding to less than two workers, when this proportion was previously one to five. So we cannot ignore reality. We have a duty to the new generation to put in place a system of insurance which will guarantee pensioners for decades. Conversion of part of the insurance system from redistributive to capitalisation I think it's not just in the right direction, but it's also a one-way road. I therefore welcome the leadership of the Ministry that boldly implements another election commitment from the New Democracy. This new system focuses on young workers who will enter the labour market from 2022 onwards. At the same time and on a voluntary basis, it is also possible for workers of up to thirty-five years' duration to join. For its implementation, the new Capital Insurance Fund, which will operate in the standards of other countries of the European Union and of the OECD, is established. In the current redistributive system, the amount of the pension is not a straightforward consequence of the amount of the insured person's contributions, but depends primarily on the number of workers who pay contributions at the time of the award of the pension. This is a misunderstanding which we have often been called upon to answer and - and rightly so in my opinion - the pensioner expects that he will take the money he has worked for life and the state has retained it. With the new system introduced by the Ministry to the draft law, that is exactly what is being achieved. The individual pigment of each insured person to which his contributions are collected shall be established. However, it is not only about saving but also about increasing investment. The money of the coin must be redeemed and this will be done through the operation of the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, which will follow tested practices, structured over proportionate studies and with a philosophy of private economy. We don't invent the wheel. We are simply applying the experience of many other countries that, through the application of a capitalisation system, have achieved an increase of 40% to 60% in the amount of supplementary pensions. Unfortunately, in our country, we have demonised, among other things, the concept of investment, as is apparent from the attitude and criticism of the Anti-Population in the draft law under discussion. That is why I particularly want to mention the provision of public guarantee for non-negative performance. What does that mean? That under no circumstances will the amount of the pension be lower than that corresponding to the contributions paid. Whether the investment goes well or not, the insured person can't get lost. The new system also strengthens transparency and accountability, as it gives more control to the insured person through the personal money monitoring system. In conclusion, Mr President, I think that this is a pioneering and innovative bill that increases confidence in the insurance system, supports growth and gives greater stability and security. It is within the framework of the authorities of the New Democracy, as it strengthens social justice and supports the worker, the farmer, the insured, the future pensioner. I therefore call on the House to vote in favour. Thank you.

In accordance with points 2287/2015 and 1889/2019 of its Plenary Sessions, the Council of State ruled out any possible capitalisation of subsidiary insurance. The highest court of the country has irrevocably ruled that, in accordance with Article 22(4) of the Constitution, subsidiary insurance is public and compulsory only provided by the State, it is foreign to the business risks associated with the exercise of the insurance function by private entities and has a final and sole guarantor of the State, which is obliged to participate in its financing. However, the draft law abolishes all this. Is Article 8 really not being circumvented by the Constitution and the case-law of the Council of State, which refers to the resources of the EAEC? The State's participation is limited only for a transitional period of five years. Article 19, liability of the members of the board of directors of the EAEC. It introduces something unprecedented in time, the criminal impunity and the complete civil immunity of the fund managers for the exercise of their duties, and the draft law prior to Article 9 entrusts the insured with the operational expenses of the TECA and the salaries of its managers. In other words, part of the payment for supplementary pensions for the future is withdrawn. So, ladies and gentlemen of the government, how then do you say that supplementary pensions will increase in relation to today's pensions? The whole opposite is going to happen. One more thing. Where is the government's concern for the demographic issue? One would expect the new insurance to give weight to the demographics, since in Greece for twenty years deaths have exceeded births, while seven hundred thousand young Greeks have migrated massively in the years of the crisis. Who will work, ladies and gentlemen, so that pensions can be guaranteed? Is it possible for a worker to correspond to a pensioner? ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u320	Thank you, Mr. Papastasis. Mr Andreas Nikolakopoulou has the floor now.
ParlaMint-GR_2021-09-01-S1-commons.u321	Thank you, Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, the bill under discussion is one of the most centralised bills, since it concerns the reform of the supplementary insurance system, and it concerns the transition to a system implemented in several advanced states of the world. It is a gradual conversion of the ancillary insurance system from redistributive to capitalisation for each new insured person, each new entrant, and for each insured person up to thirty-five years who chooses it. It is a model that will lead to higher supplementary pensions for the new insured, while ensuring that old insured persons are not affected at all that they have already established. Ladies and gentlemen, Prime Minister Kyriakos Mitsotakis, already from the International Exhibition of Thessaloniki in 2018, had put insurance reform as a basic parameter of our planning positions, a new model for Greece that should have been implemented as in other states for decades. This model has three pillars. The first pillar concerns the main basic pension, the state guarantee, which is based on a distribution system where the current insured pays the current pensioner. In the second pillar, a pillar which forms part of the new system is introduced, the individual pigeon allowing young persons to save and gain greater control of their supplementary pension. We are essentially referring to the possibility of selecting the insured person in the new Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund of an investment profile of his own. It is a pillar with purely capitalising criteria with which, in addition to the main national pension financed and still to be financed by the State, pensioners will also have their personal subsidiary pension. Regardless of investment progress, however, the final pension may not be less than that which corresponds to the total contributions paid by the worker. And only the third pillar will concern private insurance and will be optional. We are thus implementing, with this draft law, another one of our key pre-election commitments, for which we have been instructed by the Greek citizens, and we are moving one step further forward, after the important intervention of our Government by L.4670 of 2020 for the main pension, which has corrected several of the problems of the Katrugalo Law, a law that promoted work as punishment and turned the insurance system into a substantial tax system. Now with the new insurance we can clearly say that the work not only does not penalise, but ensures that every worker is able to get back all that he has worked for. This reform is obviously annoying the opposition, as the government does almost what the government proposes. It baptizes this reform as a privatisation of the insurance system. But we have said so before, if you want to criticise, to do so on the basis of fact, because how is insurance privatised since the new Capital Insurance Fund will be a legal person governed by public law? Because he's gonna invest the money in the private sector? This shall in no case dissociate the public character of the system. Ladies and gentlemen, it is well known that one of the most serious problems in our country is demographic. This problem has been particularly stressed in a European Commission report since 2015. It is a pre-existing and constantly growing issue, as our country is showing a stable and gradual and significant decline in population and at the same time a stable ageing. In 2030, in less than ten years, we will be the oldest country in the European Union, even beyond Italy. It cannot be disputed that the insurance system is affected by the demographic situation. The ratio of workers to pensioners is now 1.7 to 1, while the system was structured several decades ago, namely in 1950 at a ratio of 4 to 1. The contributions of the employees concerned are no longer saved, but they are spent on the current pensions and the young people are the losers of a system that leads to a total depreciation. The data, therefore, have changed with the result that the current workers are considered as second-class pensioners. If the reform were not to take place, it could be done by reducing pensions, as SYRIZA did fifteen times. I should just like to remind you of the reduction of the subsidiary up to 50%, the abolition of the CSCE, the retention of the retrospectives for one year and the increased contributions for health. That's what SYRIZA did. The Government of New Democracy is not doing it. Otherwise, the treatment would be done with bold words or predictions, because Mr. Tsipras at the International Exhibition of Thessaloniki in 2018 as Prime Minister of the country then said that there is no need to reduce pensions, which, of course, he did, because the abolition of the personal difference concerns pensioners who were over seventy years old. So any cost cuts will disappear over time, that is to say, the problem will be solved physically. And I am tabling the relevant statement in the Minutes then by Mr Tsipras. [[At this point Mr Andreas Nikolakopoulou presents for the minutes the above mentioned documents, which are in the archives of the Secretariat Department of the Department of Stenography and Practice of the Parliament.]] Ladies and gentlemen, we must all realise that the change in the system regulated by this draft law is extremely necessary. The new system which will start from the beginning of next year is a system followed by many advanced states. Indeed, in countries such as Sweden, Finland, the Netherlands, Denmark, the percentage of workers covered by pension schemes is more than 80%. The new subsidiary insurance remains in public. No one will receive a supplementary pension lower than that which corresponds to the contributions paid. This reform will not affect either the payment of supplementary pensions, but does not imply an increase in contributions, the amount of which remains at 6.5 to the middle of 2022 and then is 6%. The new system we have to support is based on a sustainable subsidiary insurance, a insurance that the insured's money will be invested, but also guaranteed with the benefit of the insured and the country, a reform - an incentive for the new generation that will definitively change the perception of every young person who deserves the right to pension while at the same time giving the already insured, a reform that contributes to the country's development track and improves state revenues, a reform that, for the first time in the last thirty years, attempts to change the data with a look at tomorrow, with a look at the new generation. We are therefore building the TECA, the Auxiliary Capital Insurance Fund, which will be a public fund for managing these individual accounts and their reserve.
